Hence, for each of the cases \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M\cap W = \emptyset $$\end{document}M∩W=∅, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\subsetneq M$$\end{document}W⊊M and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M\subseteq W$$\end{document}M⊆W, the graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G[M]/\mathbb {M}_{\max }(G[M])$$\end{document}G[M]/Mmax(G[M]) is a near-cograph and this holds for every prime module M of G. Theorem 4.19 implies that G is Lev-1-Ex. The latter arguments imply that the class of Lev-1-Ex is closed under the substitution-operation .

Finally, assume that G is the join or disjoint union of two Lev-1-Ex graphs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_1$$\end{document}G1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_2$$\end{document}G2. Suppose that G contains a prime module M. Hence, neither G[M] nor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{G}[M]$$\end{document}G¯[M] is connected. This directly implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M\subseteq V(G_i)$$\end{document}M⊆V(Gi) for one \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in \{1,2\}$$\end{document}i∈{1,2}. Therefore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G[M] = G_i[M]$$\end{document}G[M]=Gi[M] and thus, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G[M]/\mathbb {M}_{\max }(G[M]) = G_i[M]/\mathbb {M}_{\max }(G_i[M])$$\end{document}G[M]/Mmax(G[M])=Gi[M]/Mmax(Gi[M]). By Theorem 4.19, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G[M]/\mathbb {M}_{\max }(G[M])$$\end{document}G[M]/Mmax(G[M]) a near-cograph. As the latter arguments hold for all prime modules M of G, Theorem 4.19 implies that G is Lev-1-Ex. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

We consider now “atomic expressions” \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{T}$$\end{document}T of G that are based on the operations , , and . As already shown in Theorem 4.24, every Lev-1-Ex graph G can be written as a sequence of Lev-1-Ex graphs in which all graphs are concatenated by the -operator. We show now that every Lev-1-Ex graph can be written as a sequence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_1$$\end{document}K1’s and primitive near-cographs where the graphs are concatenated by the -, - and -operator. To be more precise:

Definition 4.25

For a graph class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}$$\end{document}G, we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathcal {G}}$$\end{document}G_ to denote that one graph from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}$$\end{document}G is taken. We denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}$$\end{document}N the set of all primitive near-cographs and by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K the set of all single-vertex graphs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_1$$\end{document}K1. Moreover, when using , we always assume that v is a vertex in H. In addition, when using , , or , we always assume that H and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H'$$\end{document}H′ are vertex-disjoint.

An atomic expression (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{T}$$\end{document}T) is an expression defined recursively by the following grammar:4An atomic expression \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{T}$$\end{document}T is valid for G if, after substituting the elements \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathcal {K}}$$\end{document}K_ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathcal {N}}$$\end{document}N_ at each step with specified graphs from the respective classes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}$$\end{document}N, the result is a graph H that is isomorphic to G. In this case, we also say that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{T}$$\end{document}T is a valid atomic expression for G. Moreover, we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{T}(G)$$\end{document}T(G) for one of the valid atomic expression for G, if it exists.

We provide now a couple of examples for graphs that have a valid atomic expression. The simplest one is provided by the single vertex graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_1$$\end{document}K1 for which both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\mathcal {K}}$$\end{document}K_ and are valid atomic expressions, illustrating that there can be multiple valid atomic expressions for the same graph.

Clearly, every graph can be written as with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K = (\{v\}, \emptyset )$$\end{document}K=({v},∅). Theorem 4.26

A graph G is Lev-1-Ex if and only if there exists a valid atomic expression \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{T}(G)$$\end{document}T(G) for G.

Proof

We prove the only-if direction by induction on the number of vertices in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G = (X,E)$$\end{document}G=(X,E). If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|X|=1$$\end{document}|X|=1, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{T}(G) = \underline{\mathcal {K}}$$\end{document}T(G)=K_ is a valid atomic expression of G. Suppose now that all Lev-1-Ex graphs G with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|X|\le n$$\end{document}|X|≤n vertices have a valid atomic expression \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{T}(G)$$\end{document}T(G) for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge 1$$\end{document}n≥1. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G = (X,E)$$\end{document}G=(X,E) be a graph with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|X| = n+1 > 1$$\end{document}|X|=n+1>1 vertices and consider the root \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho $$\end{document}ρ of the MDT \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathscr {T},\tau )$$\end{document}(T,τ) of G. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|X|>n$$\end{document}|X|>n, it follows that for the set of children \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {M}_{\max }(G) = \{M_1,\dots ,M_k\}$$\end{document}Mmax(G)={M1,⋯,Mk} of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho $$\end{document}ρ it holds that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k>1$$\end{document}k>1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|M_i|\le n$$\end{document}|Mi|≤n. By Proposition 4.14, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_i{:}{=} G[M_i]$$\end{document}Gi:=G[Mi] is Lev-1-Ex for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in \{1,\dots ,k\}$$\end{document}i∈{1,⋯,k}. By induction hypothesis, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_i$$\end{document}Gi has a valid atomic expression \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{T}(G_i)$$\end{document}T(Gi) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in \{1,\dots ,k\}$$\end{document}i∈{1,⋯,k}.

Corollary 4.27

A graph G is Lev-1-Ex if and only if there exists a valid expression for G in the grammar recursively defined by

We close this section by showing the relationship between Lev-1-Ex graphs and other graph classes, as well as their connection to the “twin-width”. A graph G is perfect if the chromatic number of every induced subgraph equals the size of the largest clique in that subgraph. It is well-known that perfect graphs are closed under the substitution operation (cf. [75, Thm 1]). By Theorem 4.24, Lev-1-Ex graphs also possess this property. Hence, it is natural to ask whether Lev-1-Ex graphs are perfect. An affirmative answer to this question is provided in Theorem 4.29. To establish this result, we first define a hole as an induced cycle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_n$$\end{document}Cn on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \ge 5$$\end{document}n≥5 vertices. The complement of a hole is called an anti-hole.

Lemma 4.28

Lev-1-Ex graphs are hole-free and anti-hole-free.

Proof

By contraposition, suppose that G contains an induced \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_n$$\end{document}Cn on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge 5$$\end{document}n≥5 vertices, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G[W]\simeq C_n$$\end{document}G[W]≃Cn for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W\subseteq V(G)$$\end{document}W⊆V(G). One easily verifies that G[W] is primitive and not a near-cograph. By Lemma 4.12, G[W] is not Lev-1-Ex and, by Proposition 4.14, G is not Lev-1-Ex. Hence, Lev-1-Ex graphs are hole-free. Assume now that G is Lev-1-Ex. By Observation 3.2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\overline{G}}$$\end{document}G¯ is Lev-1-Ex. By the latter arguments, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\overline{G}}$$\end{document}G¯ must be hole-free which implies that G is anti-hole-free. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

A graph is weakly-chordal if and only if it does not contain holes or anti-holes [81]. As shown by Hayward in [81], weakly-chordal graphs are perfect. The latter together with Lemma 4.28 implies

Theorem 4.29

Lev-1-Ex graphs are weakly-chordal and thus, perfect.

Bonnet et al. recently introduced a novel parameter called “twin-width (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{tww}\,}}$$\end{document}tww)” as a non-negative integer measuring a graphs distance to being a cograph [43] that is based one the following characterization of cographs: A graph is a cograph if it contains two vertices with the same neighborhood (called twins), identify them, and iterate this process until one ends in a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_1$$\end{document}K1 [42, 43]. This makes cographs the unique class of graphs having twin-width 0. We omit the (lengthy) formal definition of twin-width here and instead refer the reader to [43].

Proposition 4.30

If G is a Lev-1-Ex graph, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{tww}\,}}(G)\le 2$$\end{document}tww(G)≤2. There exists Lev-1-Ex graphs with twin-width equal to 2.

Proof

We first remark that [43, Thm 4.1] states that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{tww}\,}}(G)\le 2({{\,\textrm{tww}\,}}(G-v)+1)$$\end{document}tww(G)≤2(tww(G-v)+1) for all graphs G and all vertices v of G. Since every near-cograph H has, by definition, a vertex v such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H-v$$\end{document}H-v is a cograph and thus such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{tww}\,}}(H-v)=0$$\end{document}tww(H-v)=0, every near-cograph H satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{tww}\,}}(H)\le 2$$\end{document}tww(H)≤2. Furthermore note that, by [82, Thm. 3.1], we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\,\textrm{tww}\,}}(G) = \max \{&{{\,\textrm{tww}\,}}(G[M]/\mathbb {M}_{\max }(G[M]))\,: \\ &\,M \text { is a prime module of }G\}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}tww(G)=max{tww(G[M]/Mmax(G[M])):Mis a prime module ofG},for all graphs G and thus Theorem 4.19 implies that the twin-width of any Lev-1-Ex graph G equals the maximum twin-width of a set of near-cographs. As previously argued, each such near-cograph has twin-width at most 2, thus so does G. In summary, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{tww}\,}}(G)\le 2$$\end{document}tww(G)≤2 for all Lev-1-Ex graphs G.

We show now that there are Lev-1-Ex graphs for which this bound is tight. To this end, consider the graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G=(V,E)$$\end{document}G=(V,E) where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V=\{x,v_1,v_2,v_3,u_1,u_2,u_3\}$$\end{document}V={x,v1,v2,v3,u1,u2,u3} and whose edges are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{x,v_i\}$$\end{document}{x,vi} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{v_i,u_i\}$$\end{document}{vi,ui} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1,2,3$$\end{document}i=1,2,3. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G-x$$\end{document}G-x is a cograph, G is a near-cograph and Corollary 4.11 implies that G is Lev-1-Ex. We show now that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{tww}\,}}(G)=2$$\end{document}tww(G)=2. The graph G contains an asteroidal triple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u_1,u_2,u_3)$$\end{document}(u1,u2,u3), i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_1,u_2,u_3$$\end{document}u1,u2,u3 are pairwise non-adjacent vertices and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_i$$\end{document}ui and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_j$$\end{document}uj are connected by a path in G which does not contain vertices that are adjacent to the vertex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_k$$\end{document}uk, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{i,j,k\} = \{1,2,3\}$$\end{document}{i,j,k}={1,2,3} [83]. In particular, Theorem 4 in [83] implies that G is not a so-called co-comparability graph. Therefore, [84] (c.f. [85, Thm. 4.7.1]) implies that G is not a so-called permutation graph. Now Theorem 8 in [86] implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{tww}\,}}(G)\ge 2$$\end{document}tww(G)≥2. Since G is Lev-1-Ex the previous arguments imply that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\,\textrm{tww}\,}}(G)=2$$\end{document}tww(G)=2. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Summary and outlook

In this contribution, we have established a general framework for evaluating the biological feasibility of orthology graphs. In particular, we examined whether a network-based evolutionary scenario exists that allows for speciation events, represented by a 0/1-labeling of its inner vertices, that give rise to the orthologs encoded in the orthology graph. We demonstrated that for every orthology graph G, there exists a network N with a 0/1-labeling that explains G, where N has no more hybrid vertices than G has vertices (cf. Theorem 3.10). In this sense, every inferred set of orthologous gene pairs can, in principle, be represented within some network-based evolutionary scenario. However, such a scenario may be excessively complex and biologically implausible. It is therefore natural to impose constraints on the complexity of the permissible networks. To this end, we focused on level-1 networks, which, in an intuitive sense, are close to being tree-like while still allowing for occasional non-tree-like evolutionary events. We provided several characterizations of Lev-1-Ex graphs, i.e., orthology graphs that can be explained by level-1 networks. In this context, modular decomposition proved to be a useful tool for exploring the non-tree-like behavior of networks explaining a graph G, as such behavior is determined by the structural properties within the prime modules of G. In particular, G is Lev-1-Ex if and only if each primitive subgraph is a near-cograph. Clearly, if an orthology graph G lacks this structure, it would be necessary to determine the appropriate edits (removal or addition of edges) required to transform it into a Lev-1-Ex graph. The computational complexity of the underlying editing problem remain an interesting open question for future research.

We propose that modular decomposition can serve as a toolbox for further generalizations, particularly for characterizing orthology graphs that can be explained by network types beyond level-1. Specifically, if the primitive subgraphs of an orthology graph G can be explained by a given network type, then it is plausible that the entire graph G can also be explained by the same type of network. A reasonable approach to proving this assumption for certain network types is to employ prime-vertex replacement networks (cf. Definition 4.16). These networks are used to replace prime vertices in the modular decomposition tree of G with specified networks that explain the underlying primitive subgraphs. However, even the natural generalization to level-2 networks appears to be non-trivial. As illustrated in Fig. 6, additional hybridization events introduce significantly greater structural complexity to the underlying orthology graph. Nevertheless, understanding these generalizations could provide deeper insights into how hybridization affects the representation of orthology relationships.

Another important aspect concerns the relationship between orthologs and best matches. This topic has received considerable attention in recent years under the assumption of tree-like evolution [87–89]. An orthology graph that can be explained by a tree must always be a subgraph of the (reciprocal) best match graph underlying this tree [89]. Moreover, in [90] it was shown that, given the correct best match graph, it is possible to optimally correct estimates of orthologs in polynomial time to obtain a tree-explainable orthology graph. A natural question arises: how do best matches relate to orthology in the context of networks? Addressing this question requires not only a deeper understanding of orthologs but also a more detailed investigation of best matches and their relationship to orthology in network-based evolutionary scenarios.

Finally, our results open new avenues for further research in graph theory, particularly in understanding the broader mathematical properties of orthology graphs and their relationships to other graph classes or graph parameters. Investigating these connections could lead to novel insights that extend beyond the scope of orthology inference.
